## [HN-TITLE] 21. The First Transatlantic Telegraph Cable Was a Bold, Beautiful Failure

- **Source**: [https://spectrum.ieee.org/the-first-transatlantic-telegraph-cable-was-a-bold-beautiful-failure](https://spectrum.ieee.org/the-first-transatlantic-telegraph-cable-was-a-bold-beautiful-failure)
- **Site**: IEEE Spectrum
- **Author**: Allison Marsh
- **Published**: 2019-10-31
- **HN activity**: 24 points · [4 comments](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49096319)
- **Length**: 2.8K words (~13 min read)
- **Language**: en

![Tiffany's transatlantic cable souvenir. ](https://spectrum.ieee.org/media-library/tiffany-s-transatlantic-cable-souvenir.jpg?id=25590016&width=980)Hip Hip Hooray! The opening of the first transatlantic cable was celebrated with a wide range of souvenirs, including watch fobs, earrings, pendants, charms, letter openers, candlesticks, walking-stick toppers, and tabletop displays.Photo: Division of Work & Industry/National Museum of American History/Smithsonian Institution

**On 16 August 1858, Queen Victoria and U.S. president James Buchanan** exchanged telegraphic pleasantries, inaugurating the first transatlantic cable connecting British North America to [Ireland](https://spectrum.ieee.org/tag/ireland). It wasn’t exactly [instant messaging](https://spectrum.ieee.org/tag/instant-messaging): The queen’s 98-word greeting of goodwill took almost 16 hours to send through the 3,200-kilometer cable. Still, compared to packet steamships, which could take 10 days to cross the Atlantic, the cable promised a tremendous improvement in speed for urgent communications.

This milestone in [telegraphy](https://spectrum.ieee.org/tag/telegraphy) had been a long time coming. [Samuel Morse](https://ethw.org/Samuel_Morse) first suggested linking the two continents in 1840, and various attempts were made over the ensuing years. Progress on the project took off in the mid-1850s, when [U.S. entrepreneur Cyrus W. Field](https://ethw.org/Cyrus_W._Field) began investing heavily in telegraphy.

Field had made his fortune in the paper industry by the age of 34. The first [telegraph](https://spectrum.ieee.org/tag/telegraph) project he invested in was a link from St. Johns, Newfoundland, to [New York City](https://spectrum.ieee.org/tag/new-york-city), as envisioned by [Canadian engineer Frederic Newton Gisborne](https://ethw.org/Frederic_N._Gisborne). The venture never secured enough funding, but Field’s enthusiasm for telegraphy was undiminished. Over the next decade, he invested his own money and rallied other inventors and investors to form several telegraph companies.

The most audacious of these was the Atlantic Telegraph Company (ATC). Field and the English engineers [John Watkins Brett](https://atlantic-cable.com/CablePioneers/Brett/index.htm) and [Charles Tilston Bright](https://www.lindahall.org/charles-tilston-bright/), both specialists in submarine telegraphy, formed the company in 1856, with the goal of laying a transatlantic cable. The British and U.S. governments both agreed to subsidize the project.

Terrestrial telegraphy was by then well established, and several shorter [submarine cables](https://spectrum.ieee.org/tag/submarine-cables) had been deployed in Europe and the [United States](https://spectrum.ieee.org/tag/united-states). Still, the transatlantic cable’s great length posed some unique challenges, especially because transmission theory and cable design were still very much under debate.

Morse and [British physicist Michael Faraday](https://ethw.org/Michael_Faraday) believed that the conducting wire of a [submarine cable](https://spectrum.ieee.org/tag/submarine-cable) should be as narrow as possible, to limit retardation of the signal. And the wider the wire, the more electricity would be needed to charge it. [Edward Orange Wildman Whitehouse](https://atlantic-cable.com/Books/Whitehouse/eoww.htm), an electrician for the Atlantic Telegraph Company, subscribed to this view.

The other school of thought was represented by [William Thomson (later Lord Kelvin)](https://ethw.org/William_Thomson). He argued that the amount of retardation was inversely proportional to the square of the cable’s length. Thomson suggested using a large-diameter core made with the purest copper available in order to reduce the resistance. Bright, the project’s chief engineer, shared Thomson’s view. This design was significantly heavier and more costly than the one proposed by the Morse-Faraday school, and the ATC did not adopt it.

The Gutta Percha Co. manufactured the cable’s core and insulation. The core consisted of seven strands of copper wire twisted together to make a wire 0.083 inch in diameter. The finished core weighed 107 pounds per nautical mile, which was significantly lighter than the 392 pounds per nautical mile that Thomson and Bright had proposed. The copper core was wrapped in three layers of gutta-percha, a latex that comes from trees of the same name. The insulated core was then covered in tarred hemp and wrapped with iron wire. The finished cable was about five-eighths of an inch in diameter.

![etching](https://spectrum.ieee.org/media-library/etching.jpg?id=25590017&width=980)This Mortal Coil: Cable on the HMS Agamemnon was used to lay the first transatlantic telegraph line, which began operating in 1858.Photo: Hulton Archive/Getty Images

At the time, no ship could carry all of the submarine cable needed, so the cargo was split between two naval ships, the HMS *Agamemnon* and the USSF *Niagara*, both of which were refitted to carry the load. It took three weeks to load the cable. Many spectators gathered to watch, while local officials and visiting dignitaries treated the naval officers to countless dinners and celebrations, much of which was recorded and amplified by the press.

Of course, two ships meant that the cables would have to be spliced together at some point. Once again, there was disagreement about how to proceed.

Bright argued for splicing the cable in midocean and then having each ship head in opposite directions, paying out its cable as it went. Whitehouse and the other electricians preferred to begin laying the cable in Ireland and splicing in the second half once the first half had been laid. This plan would allow continuous contact with the shore and ongoing testing of the cable’s signal. Bright’s plan had the advantage of halving the time to lay the cable, thus lessening the chance of encountering foul weather.

The directors initially chose Whitehouse’s plan. *Niagara* and *Agamemnon* met at Queenstown, Ireland, to test the cable with a temporary splice. After a successful transmission, the ships headed to Valentia Bay to begin their mission, escorted by the USS *Susquehanna* and the HMS *Leopard*. Also joining the fleet were the support ships HMS *Advice*, HMS *Willing Mind*, and HMS *Cyclops*.

On 5 August 1857, the expedition got under way. The first portion of cable to be laid was known as the shore cable: heavily reinforced line to guard against strains of waves, currents, rocks, and anchors. But less than 5 miles out, the shore cable got caught in the machinery and broke. The fleet returned to port.

*Willing Mind* ran a thick rope to retrieve the broken shore cable, and crew members spliced it back to the shore cable on the *Niagara.* The fleet set out again. When they reached the end of the shore cable, the crews spliced it to the ocean cable and slowly lowered it to the ocean floor.

For the next few days, the cable laying proceeded. There was nearly continuous communication between Whitehouse on shore and Field, Morse, and Thomson on board, although Morse was incapacitated by seasickness much of the time.

The paying-out machinery was fiddly to operate. The cable was occasionally thrown off the wheel, and tar from the cable built up in the grooves and had to be cleaned off. To keep the cable paying out at a controlled rate required constant adjustment of the machinery’s brakes. The brakeman had to balance the ship’s speed and the ocean current. In perfect weather and flat seas, this was easy to judge. But weather can be fickle, and humans are fallible.

![map](https://spectrum.ieee.org/media-library/map.jpg?id=25590018&width=980)Charted Waters: The transatlantic cable connected Heart’s Content, in Newfoundland, to Telegraph Field on Valentia Island, in Ireland. The cable’s chief enthusiast, Cyrus W. Field, is shown midocean.Photo: PhotoQuest/Getty Images

Around 3:45 a.m. on 11 August, the stern of *Niagara* headed into the trough of a wave. As the ship rose, the pressure on the cable increased. The brakes should have been released, but they weren’t. The cable broke and plunged to an irretrievable depth.

Field immediately headed to England aboard the *Leopard* to meet with the ATC’s board of directors. *Niagara* and *Agamemnon* remained at the site for a few days to practice splicing the cable from the two ships. *Cyclops,* which had done the initial survey of the route the previous year, conducted soundings of the site. When they returned to shore, the crews learned that the project had been halted for the year.

**Over the winter months,** William Everett was named chief engineer and set about redesigning the paying-out machinery with more attention to the braking mechanism and safety features. The crew practiced their maneuvers. Thomson thought more about transmission speed and developed his mirrored galvanometer, an instrument for detecting current in, say, a long cable.

The ships set out again the following summer. This time they would follow Bright’s plan. *Agamemnon* and *Niagara* would meet at 52°2’ N, 33°18’ W, the halfway point of the proposed line. In the middle of the Atlantic Ocean, they would splice together the cable and drop it to the ocean floor. *Agamemnon* would head east to Ireland, while *Niagara* headed west to Newfoundland.

Although the weather was fine when the ships set out, it soon turned. For six days, the two ships, laden with 1,500 tons of cable, pitched alarmingly from side to side. Although no one was lost, 45 men were injured, and *Agamemnon* ended up 200 miles off course.

Finally, on 25 June 1858 *Agamemnon* and *Niagara* met. The crews spliced together the cable, and the ships set off. At first, the two ships were able to communicate via the cable, but around 3:30 a.m. on 27 June, both logbooks recorded a failure. Because things looked fine on each ship, the crews assumed the problem was on the other end, and the ships returned to the rendezvous spot. The crews didn’t want to waste time investigating, so they agreed to abandon the 100 km of cable that had been laid and spliced together a new one, and the ships set off once more.

By 29 June, *Agamemnon* had paid out almost all of the cable stored on deck, which meant the crew would have to switch to the main coil in the middle of the night. Although they had practiced the maneuver over the winter, luck was not on their side. Around midnight, the cable snapped and was lost. As it turned out, the six-day storm had damaged the cable as it lay on the deck. The two ships were hundreds of kilometers apart by this point, and they headed back to Queenstown to await further direction.

Field was not deterred, but it took some doing to convince the rest of the ATC’s board of directors to make another attempt. He could be a persuasive guy.

The ships set out for a third time on 17 July 1858. This time the cable laying progressed without incident, having been blessed, finally, by the weather gods. On 29 July, as Field recorded in his journal, the two ships spliced the two ends of the cable together in the middle of the Atlantic Ocean, dropped it in the water at 1,500 fathoms (2,745 meters), and then each ship headed to its destination port. *Niagara* arrived on 4 August, *Agamemnon* the following day. The 3,200-km cable now connected Bay Bulls Arm (which was renamed Sunnyside in 1921), in Newfoundland, to Telegraph Field on Valentia Island, in Ireland.

By 10 August dispatchers were sending test messages, and on 16 August, with the queen’s and Buchanan’s exchanges, the line was officially open.

**All of the project’s many starts and stops** had been followed closely [by the press](https://atlantic-cable.com/Article/1858Leslies/index.htm) and an eager public. Archaeologist [Cassie](https://robotsguide.com/robots/cassie) Newland has called the heroic effort “[the Victorian equivalent of the Apollo mission](https://theconversation.com/how-the-victorians-cheated-time-and-space-by-laying-the-first-trans-atlantic-telegraphy-cable-65837).”

The triumphant opening, after years of speculation and so many failures**,** was lauded as the communications achievement of the century. New York City celebrated with a parade and fireworks, which accidentally set the dome of City Hall on fire. [Trinity Church in lower Manhattan held a special service](https://anglicanhistory.org/usa/gwdoane/telegraph1858.html) to commemorate the cable, with the mayor of New York City and other officials in attendance and the Right Reverend George Washington Doane, Bishop of New Jersey, giving the address. On the other side of the Atlantic, shares in the ATC more than doubled, and Charles Bright was knighted for his work overseeing the project.

Of course, companies wanted to cash in on the celebration and immediately crafted all sorts of memorabilia and souvenirs. *Niagara* had arrived in New York with hundreds of kilometers of excess cable. The jeweler Tiffany & Co. bought it all.

Tiffany craftsmen cut the cable into 10-centimeter pieces, banding the ends of each piece with brass ferrules and attaching a descriptive plate \[see photo at top]. The souvenirs retailed for 50 cents each (about US $15 today); wholesalers could buy lots of 100 for $25. Each piece came with a facsimile of a note, signed by Cyrus W. Field, certifying that he had sold the balance of the cable from *Niagara* to Tiffany. Although Tiffany claimed to have a monopoly on the cable, numerous competitors sprang up with [their own cable souvenirs](https://atlantic-cable.com/Souvenirs/index.htm), including watch fobs, earrings, pendants, charms, letter openers, candlesticks, walking-stick toppers, and tabletop displays.

![img](https://spectrum.ieee.org/media-library/img.jpg?id=25590019&width=980)Irrational Exuberance: Many souvenirs commemorated the heroic completion of the first transatlantic cable. Some, like this one and the one at top, incorporated actual pieces of leftover cable.Photo: SSPL/Getty Images

Tiffany reportedly sold thousands of its cable souvenirs, but it was a short-lived venture. Transmission on the transatlantic cable, never very strong, degraded quickly. Within a few weeks, the line failed entirely.

Blame for the failure quickly landed on Whitehouse, chief engineer for the eastern terminus of the cable. He believed that the farther the signal had to travel, the stronger the necessary voltage, and so he at times used up to 2,000 volts to try to boost the signal. Meanwhile, Thomson, the chief engineer for the cable’s western terminus, was using his mirror galvanometer to detect and amplify the faint signal coming through the cable.

In blaming Whitehouse for the failure, people were quick to point out his lack of traditional qualifications. This is a bit unfair—his path was similar to that of many gentleman scientists of the time. Trained as a medical doctor, he had a successful surgical practice before turning his attention to electrical experiments. Whitehouse patented several improvements to telegraphic apparatus and was elected a member of the British Association for the Advancement of Science. But the commission investigating the cable’s failure laid fault on Whitehouse’s use of high voltages.

In 1985 historian and engineer [Donard de Cogan published an article](https://atlantic-cable.com/Books/Whitehouse/DDC/) that somewhat vindicated Whitehouse. De Cogan’s analysis of a length of cable that had been retrieved from the original deployment noted its poor manufacture, including the fact that the copper core was not centered within the insulator and at places was perilously close to the metal sheathing. Additionally, there was significant deterioration of the gutta percha insulator. De Cogan speculated that the impurities—which even Thomson objected to—along with improper storage over the 1857–58 winter resulted in a cable whose failure was inevitable. De Cogan also concluded, though, that having Whitehouse as a scapegoat may have actually helped advance transoceanic telegraphy. Had the cable failed without a cause, investors would have been more hesitant.

Regardless, the failure left Tiffany with thousands of unsellable souvenirs. Some ended up [in museum collections](https://www.nyhistory.org/exhibit/sections-atlantic-telegraph-cable-930-original-packing-crates-8), but many were put into storage and forgotten, leading to the next twist in this tale.

**In 1974 a company called Lanello Reserves** advertised the sale of 2,000 Tiffany cable souvenirs. The asking price was $100—about $500 in today’s dollars. Lanello Reserves also donated 100 pieces to [the Smithsonian Institution](https://americanhistory.si.edu/collections/search/object/nmah_1255864), which the museum resold in its shops. Today, original transatlantic cable souvenirs pop up regularly in online auction sites. You too can own a piece of history.

While the souvenirs may have had a longer life than the transmission cable itself, that doesn’t diminish the accomplishment of Bright, Thomson, Whitehouse, and their teams. Even though the cable never worked well, a total of 732 messages were sent before it failed. These included the reporting of a collision between the Cunard Line ships [*Europa*](https://www.norwayheritage.com/p_ship.asp?sh=euro0) and [*Arabia*](https://www.norwayheritage.com/p_ship.asp?sh=euro0), as well as an order from the British government to hold two regiments in [Canada](https://spectrum.ieee.org/tag/canada)*.* The regiments were en route to [India](https://spectrum.ieee.org/tag/india), but when the British government learned that the Indian Rebellion had been repressed, they sent new orders via the cable. By not sending the troops, the treasury saved an estimated £50,000 to £60,000, recouping about one-seventh of their investment in the cable with a single military order.

Public sentiment toward the cable quickly cooled, however. By the end of 1858, rumors abounded that this was all an elaborate hoax or a fraudulent stock scheme aimed at fleecing unsuspecting investors. Similar to today’s conspiracy theorists who refuse to believe that the [Apollo](https://spectrum.ieee.org/tag/apollo) [moon](https://spectrum.ieee.org/tag/moon) landing was real, the cable doubters were not convinced by souvenirs, messages from heads of state, or effusive press coverage.

Field was eager to try again. Most of the ATC’s original financial backers weren’t interested in investing in another transatlantic cable, but the British government still saw the potential and continued to provide funding. The company finally succeeded in building a [permanent transatlantic cable](https://ethw.org/Milestones:Landing_of_the_Transatlantic_Cable,_1866) in 1866.

Field may have lost many of his friends and investors, but he never lost his optimism. He was able to believe in a future of instant communication at a time when most people didn’t have—indeed, couldn’t even conceive of—indoor plumbing and electric lights.

*An abridged version of this article appears in the November 2019 print issue as “Tiffany’s Transatlantic Telegraphy Doodad.”*

*Part of a* [*continuing series*](https://spectrum.ieee.org/tag/past+forward) *looking at photographs of historical artifacts that embrace the boundless potential of technology.*

*A correction to this article was made on 1 February 2021.*

## About the Author

Allison Marsh is an associate professor of history at the University of South Carolina and codirector of the university’s Ann Johnson Institute for Science, Technology & Society.

## [HN-TITLE] 22. Dubious research tied to Red Bull has shaped energy drink policy

- **Source**: [https://www.theexamination.org/articles/red-bull-funded-research-energy-drinks-alcohol](https://www.theexamination.org/articles/red-bull-funded-research-energy-drinks-alcohol)
- **Site**: The Examination
- **Author**: Sasha Chavkin, Ashley Okwuosa, Fernanda Aguirre, Romina Colman
- **Published**: 2026-07-30
- **HN activity**: 122 points · [185 comments](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49124738)
- **Length**: 3.6K words (~16 min read)
- **Language**: en

*This story, co-published by* [*STAT*](https://www.statnews.com/)*, is part of the Behind the Buzz project, an investigative collaboration involving* [*The Examination*](https://www.theexamination.org/)*,* [*The Bristol Cable*](https://thebristolcable.org/)*,* [*Daraj*](https://daraj.media/en/)*,* [*Investico*](https://www.platform-investico.nl/)*,* [*Paper Trail Media*](https://www.papertrailmedia.de/)*,* [*Der Spiegel*](https://www.spiegel.de/panorama/gesellschaft/opioid-krise-in-deutschland-sucht-auf-rezept-a-8e5a7d4c-2b2e-4597-9e7a-2e6eccec7032?giftToken=31b1ea17-7c07-4786-9ee1-67c00450cb40)*,* [*Der Standard*](https://www.derstandard.at/consent/tcf/story/3000000236176/opium-fuer-die-schmerzen-warum-die-opioidkrise-oesterreich-nicht-verschont-hat) *and the* [*Toronto Star*](https://www.thestar.com/?utm_source=ig&utm_medium=social&utm_content=link_in_bio&fbclid=PAZXh0bgNhZW0CMTEAc3J0YwZhcHBfaWQPOTM2NjE5NzQzMzkyNDU5AAGnN7Z7OPY-3MPbxnlQoDfBMws6juIzaEocbAJNcSUhZZQY8EGxGHp2I8ySLtc_aem_vRPKPwTZ-89sCWqoVL_omQ)*.*

On a winter morning in a Long Island town about two hours east of New York City’s skyscrapers, a Dutch researcher told local officials that their concerns about mixing heavily caffeinated energy drinks with alcohol were overblown.

A few months earlier, the U.S. Food and Drug Administration had effectively banned Four Loko, a caffeinated malt liquor dubbed “blackout in a can” by college students. Suburban Suffolk County was now considering something more sweeping: a ban on selling energy drinks to minors.

Joris Verster, an academic from Utrecht University, said there was no scientific basis for the law. Though booze and energy drinks are often consumed together, Verster argued that beverages like Red Bull aren’t to blame for bad things that happen when people get drunk. 

He offered an analogy that might resonate with locals; Suffolk County includes the beach towns that inspired the thriller “Jaws.” Blaming energy drinks for binge drinking and alcohol-fueled injuries, he said, would be like blaming shark attacks on summertime ice cream sales.

“The shark attacks are not caused by the ice cream sales,” Verster said in the February 2011 hearing. 

He was one of three scientists who had crossed the Atlantic to urge officials to drop the proposal. The others were a British professor and Red Bull’s chief scientific officer. The trio shared more than a research interest: Through paychecks or research grants, all three had gotten money from Red Bull, the world’s largest energy drink company.

Their argument won the day. Lawmakers dropped the age limit, marking an early victory for the Austria-based company in what would become a global campaign to use science to combat restrictions on its products.

For more than a decade, Red Bull paid hundreds of thousands of dollars for university professors to conduct research that has been favorable to the energy drink industry, a joint investigation involving The Examination, The Bristol Cable, Daraj, Investico, Paper Trail Media, Der Spiegel, Der Standard, STAT and the Toronto Star found. The company has flown academics to U.S. cities and provided talking points for meetings with government officials who were considering restrictions on energy drinks. It funded a doctorate for a researcher in the United Kingdom and another in the Netherlands. 

In the United States and Canada, research backed by Red Bull or conducted by academics with financial ties to the company has been used to defeat measures such as age restrictions and warning labels. 

A pivotal report by a European food agency in 2015 cited these studies in finding that there wasn’t clear evidence that mixing energy drinks and alcohol was harmful. That report remains the last word in Europe and has been used to prevent action elsewhere.

The studies backed by Red Bull have been nearly unanimous in their message: Consuming energy drinks and alcohol together is no more harmful than drinking alcohol alone. 

That stands in stark contrast to the vast majority of independent studies on the subject.

[The Examination gathered more than 100 studies](https://www.theexamination.org/articles/red-bull-alcohol-research-methodology) about the health or behavioral risks of mixing energy drinks and alcohol. Of the studies that received funding by Red Bull or were conducted by researchers who disclosed financial conflicts of interest with the company, 95% concluded that mixing alcohol with energy drinks did not increase those risks. Roughly 80% of independent studies reached the opposite conclusion, The Examination found.

Companies often fund research into controversial products, a practice that is criticized by health advocates. But leading alcohol researchers who reviewed some of the studies with financial ties to Red Bull said there are deeper problems.

A number of the studies are flawed, they said, raising questions about their conclusions. In Boston, a team of scientists withdrew from a Red Bull-funded study after Verster, the Dutch researcher, refused to address problems they believed would bias the results.

In a brief statement to The Examination and its partners, Karlheinz Niederreiter, Global Head of Science, Regulatory and Government Affairs at Red Bull, wrote, “Health authorities across the world have concluded that Red Bull Energy Drink is safe to consume.” That’s why, he wrote, nearly 14 billion cans were sold worldwide last year. 

Niederreiter and other Red Bull executives did not answer questions about the news outlets’ findings, including those about the safety of consuming energy drinks with alcohol.

[Voluntary guidelines published by American](https://aba-bigtree.s3.amazonaws.com/files/resources/abaguidancefortheresponsiblelabelingandmarketingof.pdf) and [European industry groups](https://www.energydrinkseurope.org/wp-content/uploads/2022/06/EDE-Code-of-Practice_update-2022_FINAL.pdf) say their member companies, which include Red Bull and Monster Beverage Corp., don’t promote mixing energy drinks with alcohol and don’t claim that energy drinks counteract the effects of alcohol. The European group said academic research has found that mixing the two doesn’t pose additional risks.

Peter Miller, a professor at Deakin University in Australia, said the news outlets’ findings weren’t surprising. Back in 2013, around the time that much of this research was published, he wrote an [essay in a medical journal](https://www.bmj.com/content/347/bmj.f5345#ref-1/) criticizing the wave of industry-funded studies about mixing energy drinks and alcohol. 

Those research grants are “an investment using universities’ reputation to be able to influence policy,” Miller told The Examination. “This is a campaign to protect their profits and to avoid legislative intervention.”

And it’s worked, he said. Red Bull-funded researchers have “absolutely undermined the evidence base,” Miller said. “It leads to this doubt where politicians don't act.”

Much of this research was conducted at a time of growing public debate about the safety of energy drinks. Especially for young people. And especially in combination with alcohol.

## **Wide-awake drunk**

![](https://www.theexamination.org/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fa.storyblok.com%2Ff%2F212662%2F1920x1080%2F628b902cac%2Fbeverages_story-1_coroner-report.png&w=3840&q=75)

As concerns intensified, the U.S. Food and Drug Administration logged reports of heart problems and other adverse events allegedly related to energy drinks. In Quebec, Canada, a coroner concluded that 21-year-old Zachary Mitchell drowned after a probable arrhythmia linked to exercise and energy drink consumption.Illustration by The Examination/Evident Media

One weekend night in August 2016, Zachary Mitchell joined some friends for a getaway at a lakeside cottage in Quebec. The 21-year-old visited the cottage nearly every summer weekend, his mother said.

Mitchell drank several cans of Red Bull and about six beers over about two hours before he and his friends decided to go for a swim, according to a coroner's report.

Mitchell had been born near a lake and learned to swim from a young age. “He was a fish,” said his mother, Heather Mitchell.

![](https://www.theexamination.org/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fa.storyblok.com%2Ff%2F212662%2F1366x768%2F4d7f35a1bf%2Fbeverages_story-1_-zachary-mitchell-portrait-with-gradiant.png&w=3840&q=75)

Zachary Mitchell, shown at his high school graduation in 2012.Photo provided by Heather Mitchell

But shortly before midnight, Mitchell hit the water awkwardly when he dove from a 15-foot high deck. He briefly resurfaced, spoke a few words and went under again. That was the last his friends saw of him, according to a coroner's report.

The coroner wrote that Mitchell had drowned, likely due to an arrhythmia triggered by the combination of energy drinks and physical exercise. She noted that he had a blood-alcohol content of about 0.1%, slightly over the legal limit for driving. 

In her report, she called for a “vast advertising awareness campaign targeting a young audience” about the risks of energy drinks, including those from combining them with alcohol.

![](https://www.theexamination.org/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fa.storyblok.com%2Ff%2F212662%2F1366x768%2F7e02afe8f3%2Fbeverages_story-1_-zachary-mitchell-swim.png&w=3840&q=75)

Mitchell, pictured diving into Lake Saint Pierre in Quebec on another occasion, grew up around the water and was a strong swimmer, his mother, Heather Mitchell, recalled. “He was a fish,” she said.Photos provided by Heather Mitchell

Researchers have a term that captures their concern about mixing alcohol and energy drinks: wide-awake drunk.

Tim Stockwell, who headed the Canadian Institute for Substance Use Research from 2004 to 2020, said adding the stimulation of energy drinks to the impaired judgment caused by alcohol is a recipe for risky behavior. 

The caffeine in energy drinks ranges from 80 mg in a Red Bull, slightly less than a typical cup of coffee, to 300 mg in other brands. But energy drinks also contain ingredients such as taurine and guarana that can magnify its effects on the body.

“It's a pretty potentially dangerous combination, giving you the energy to do crazy, daft things,” Stockwell said, “and something that takes away the fear of the consequences.”

People have been hurt or killed all over the world after consuming alcohol and energy drinks, according to news reports. In England, an 18-year-old woman [suffered three cardiac arrests](https://www.thetimes.com/life-style/health-fitness/article/jagerbomb-teenager-suffered-heart-attacks-55hbjh6dfj3) after downing 10 Jägerbombs, a cocktail that combines an energy drink with the spirit Jägermeister. In Washington state, a 17-year-old boy [died of acute alcohol intoxication](https://patch.com/washington/edmonds/alcohol-intoxication-cause-of-death-of-snohomish-county-teen) after mixing hard liquor and energy drinks while playing video games with friends. In Quebec, a 14-year-old girl [was found dead behind her school](https://www.cbc.ca/news/canada/ottawa/gatineau-youth-support-decision-to-pull-controversial-drink-after-teen-s-death-1.4561091) after consuming a caffeinated alcoholic drink called FCKD UP.

A considerable body of research suggests that incidents like these aren’t just chance or circumstance. Mixing alcohol and energy drinks [was linked to higher rates of binge drinking, sexual abuse and drunk driving](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/18439201/), according to a study conducted among U.S. college students. Another[](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6907685/) [study of hospital patients in Canada](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6907685/) found the combination was tied to higher levels of injury; among women, the risk of injury tripled.

Health authorities have shared these views. The[](https://www.cdc.gov/alcohol/about-alcohol-use/alcohol-caffeine.html) [U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention warn](https://www.cdc.gov/alcohol/about-alcohol-use/alcohol-caffeine.html) that mixing alcohol and energy drinks can lead to more drinking and a greater risk of injury, drunk driving and unwanted or unprotected sex.

This research was just emerging back in 2010 during a string of deaths involving [young people](https://abcnews.com/US/loko-removes-caffeine-energy-drink/story?id=12167659) and Four Loko. [The FDA acted quickly](https://www.nytimes.com/2010/11/16/us/16drinks.html), ordering Four Loko and other companies to stop selling caffeinated alcoholic drinks.

At the time of Mitchell’s death, energy drinks in Canada carried a warning saying not to mix them with alcohol. The coroner investigating his death criticized those warnings, writing that they were “insufficient” and “in small print.”

Heather Mitchell said that neither she nor her son were fully aware of the risks of energy drinks. She said she really didn’t know if he frequently drank them. She assumed that if they were on the shelf, they were safe.

## **Red Bull gets into the research business**

![](https://www.theexamination.org/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fa.storyblok.com%2Ff%2F212662%2F1920x1080%2F7fe491c305%2Fbeverages_story-1_red-bull-research-business.jpg&w=3840&q=75)

Red Bull funded a series of studies, many co-authored by Verster at Utrecht University, that found that mixing alcohol and energy drinks did not increase drinking or harm. Other researchers questioned the studies’ validity.Illustration by The Examination/Evident Media

In 2011, soon after becoming director of an injury prevention research center at Boston University, Jonathan Howland joined two Red Bull executives for dinner. One of them was Niederreiter, who was in charge of science for the company. Lobster was on the menu and a scientific collaboration was taking shape.

Howland, who believed the FDA had overstepped the evidence with its ban on caffeinated alcoholic beverages, had taken note of ongoing research by Verster, the Dutch professor. The two ran in similar academic circles; both were members of a small group of researchers who studied alcohol hangovers. Verster’s preliminary findings suggested that mixing alcohol and energy drinks did not cause people to drink more or suffer higher rates of harm.

When Verster said he wanted to replicate his study in the United States, Howland didn’t hesitate. “I just jumped on it,” he said in an interview. That led to a deal between Red Bull and Boston University.

The key to Verster’s research was that he didn’t look for differences between people who drank only alcohol and people who consumed both alcohol and energy drinks, Verster told The Examination. Instead, he built his studies around people who did both, comparing outcomes between when they consumed alcohol alone and when they consumed it with energy drinks.

This eliminated variables such as personality type and enabled him to test for causality, Verster said.

But several months after Howland and his team started the project, they discovered a problem in how the study was set up. The details of the dispute were laid out in emails, study documents, and interviews with Howland and Tamara Calise, who consulted with the Boston team as a research expert.

The Boston researchers were alarmed because the survey that Verster’s team had provided defined alcohol mixed with energy drinks to include only alcoholic beverages consumed within two hours of an energy drink. That could underestimate binge drinking and harm linked to energy drinks, Howland’s team determined. There was no restriction on which drinks were counted when participants drank alcohol alone.

Howland and his colleagues urged Verster to change his approach. Verster refused. Howland said the message was clear: that “if you want the money, you do it exactly the way we tell you.”

Howland's team ultimately decided to back out of the study.

“We decided that we cannot use our university positions and professional reputations to recruit for a study that we believe cannot test important hypotheses in an unbiased way,” Howland wrote to Verster in June 2012.

![](https://www.theexamination.org/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fa.storyblok.com%2Ff%2F212662%2F640x480%2Feec801256c%2Fbeverages_story-1_howland.JPG&w=3840&q=75)

Jonathan Howland (left), and Verster (third from right) were both members of a group of academics who studied alcohol hangovers. Here, members of that group and their guests, including Howland’s wife Elizabeth (right) eat lobster in Nova Scotia as part of a meeting in 2012.Photo provided to The Examination

That didn’t seem to affect Red Bull’s support of Verster’s research. Later that year, he and four colleagues [published a study of Dutch students](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3508567/), funded by the company, that used the disputed approach and came to a surprising conclusion: People were better off when they mixed alcohol with energy drinks than when they drank alcohol alone. When they combined the two, they drank less alcohol and were less likely to suffer harmful consequences, the study concluded.

Five alcohol researchers consulted by The Examination said they agreed with the Boston researchers that the approach by Verster’s team was flawed. The half-life of caffeine is [five to six hours](https://archive.cdc.gov/www_cdc_gov/niosh/emres/longhourstraining/caffeine.html) — meaning that five to six hours after someone ingests caffeine, half of it remains in their system and continues to affect them. That’s significantly longer than the time window used by Verster.

From 2016 to 2021, Verster and other Red Bull-funded scientists in the U.K. and Australia published at least five more studies that used the same definition of mixing alcohol and energy drinks. All concluded that mixing did not lead to increased drinking or harm. 

Laura Schmidt, a professor at the University of California, San Francisco who has examined research funded by the food and beverage industry, described those studies as “marketing dressed up as science.” 

William Kerr, scientific director at the federally funded National Alcohol Research Center, said, “Aspects of the studies seem biased to deliver counterintuitive results that support industry perspectives.” 

### We decided that we cannot use our university positions and professional reputations to recruit for a study that we believe cannot test important hypotheses in an unbiased way.

Jonathan Howland, in an email to Joris Verster in June 2012

In an email to The Examination, Verster acknowledged that the Boston researchers raised questions about the study methodology. He said he didn’t change it because he wanted the study to be comparable to his earlier research.

Regarding his definition of mixing of alcoholic and energy drinks, he wrote that the two-hour limit was meant to exclude energy drinks consumed much earlier in the day, not to limit the number of alcoholic drinks counted.

### Subscribe to our newsletter

Global health reporting, straight to your inbox

Utrecht University investigated potential conflicts of interest in Verster’s partnership with Red Bull more than a decade ago. The university determined that Verster was insufficiently transparent because he had been paid through his own company for studies that cited his university affiliation. However, the university concluded this wasn’t a breach of integrity. 

After recent inquiries by the Dutch news outlet Investico about Verster’s studies, the university said that Verster had run afoul of its policy governing outside-funded research. The university said it didn’t know the extent of that work and that it was a problem for Verster to continue to be paid privately while citing his university affiliation.

The university said the inquiries had “prompted internal questions” and that it was setting up a committee to investigate the “scientific culture and scientific quality policy” within Verster’s research group.

Verster, the most prolific among the Red Bull-backed researchers, denied that he violated ethical guidelines and said he stands by his research. He chalked up criticisms of his work to scientific disagreements.

Sean Johnson, whose doctorate was funded by Red Bull and who published several studies with Verster, has expressed reservations about how the Red Bull-funded research was conducted, the U.K.’s Bristol Cable has found.

Johnson reflected on his early research in a commentary submitted in 2023 as part of his doctoral submission. He wrote that research on mixing alcohol and energy drinks seemed to have become politicized; at times, going through the peer review process “felt like you were in the trenches.” Later, his perspective on that period shifted.

“I may have developed an unconscious bias towards my academic group and industry funders position,” Johnson wrote. In parentheses after “funders,” he wrote, “Red Bull GmbH.”

Johnson did not respond to repeated requests for comment.

## **‘Science is science, regardless of who is funding it’**

![](https://www.theexamination.org/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fa.storyblok.com%2Ff%2F212662%2F1920x1080%2F05d21491bb%2Fbeverages_story-1_efsa.png&w=3840&q=75)

The European Food Safety Authority cited research by scientists with financial ties to Red Bull in deciding that mixing alcohol and energy drinks wasn’t a serious health concern. The food authority’s report was later cited by Health Canada in deciding to drop a requirement that energy drink cans warn people against consuming them with alcohol.Illustration by The Examination/Evident Media

The number of studies with financial ties to Red Bull about the risks of alcohol and energy drinks is relatively small, but that body of research has been influential nonetheless. Reports on energy drink policy by government or advisory bodies in the [United Kingdom](https://webarchive.nationalarchives.gov.uk/ukgwa/20200808010029/https://cot.food.gov.uk/cotstatements/cotstatementsyrs/cotstatements2012/cotstatement201204), [Canada](https://www.publichealthontario.ca/-/media/Documents/E/2017/eb-ameds.pdf), [Australia](http://web.archive.org/web/20250706025338/https://www.health.nsw.gov.au/aod/resources/Publications/alcohol-and-energy-drinks-in-nsw.pdf), [Spain](https://www.aesan.gob.es/AECOSAN/docs/documentos/seguridad_alimentaria/evaluacion_riesgos/informes_cc_ingles/ENERGY_DRINKS_RIKS.pdf) and [Ireland](http://web.archive.org/web/20230930182545/https://www.safefood.net/getattachment/21426e3b-754f-4101-85ea-499e1e76cacf/Energy-Drinks-15-March-with-cover.pdf?lang=en-IE) have cited these studies, The Examination found.

The most significant was a report by the European Food Safety Authority. As the European Union’s scientific adviser on food issues, its opinions not only inform policy in Europe, but also carry weight internationally. 

In 2015, that agency released a sweeping report that broadly endorsed the safety of caffeine and energy drinks. Three sections addressing the combination of energy drinks and alcohol cited Verster’s studies. 

The most detailed discussion was drawn from a 2014 study authored by Verster and three colleagues who had previously received Red Bull funding. They reviewed laboratory studies to determine whether mixing alcohol and caffeine caused people to get drunk without realizing it, a phenomenon known as “masking.” They concluded that masking was not a threat.

Though masking was a subject of research at the time, several alcohol researchers told The Examination, it’s not the most serious issue when it comes to energy drinks and alcohol. Far more significant, they said, is that [energy drinks can prime people to drink more alcohol](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22724427/) and that the combination of the two creates a sense of excitement and disinhibition — “wide-awake drunk.”

The European food authority’s report echoed Verster and his colleagues’ conclusion that masking — and more generally, mixing alcohol and energy drinks — was not a serious concern. The food authority’s report didn’t note that the studies disclosed researchers’ ties to Red Bull. 

Silvia Valtueña Martínez, a European Food Safety Authority scientist who contributed to its 2015 report, defended its findings. The authors considered a wide range of research, she said, and industry-backed studies like Red Bull’s should not be discounted. 

“Science is science, regardless of who is funding it,” she said.

Although the food authority recently said it would revisit its findings on caffeine and energy drinks, Malta Today reported that it [doesn’t plan to address the risks of mixing energy drinks and alcohol](https://www.maltatoday.com.mt/news/europe/142845/european_food_safety_authority_to_update_scientific_stance_on_caffeine_for_children_and_youths).

For more than a decade, the food authority’s report has been a key reference regarding energy drinks in Europe and beyond; government and advisory bodies in the United Kingdom, Canada, Germany and other countries have cited it.

So too has the beverage industry, using it to argue against regulations on energy drinks. 

### Science is science, regardless of who is funding it.

Silvia Valtueña Martínez, European Food Safety Authority scientist

One of those efforts was in Canada, several years after Mitchell’s death.

The alcohol warning label that was on energy drink cans when he died was the result of a temporary measure. In 2021, Health Canada, the country’s public health agency, proposed making the warning permanent, noting that mixing alcohol with energy drinks had been “identified as a public health concern.” 

The beverage industry said there was no scientific basis for the warning label — and it had research to back up its position.

The Canadian Beverage Association [sent Health Canada a letter citing three studies](https://www.documentcloud.org/documents/28519522-cba-letter-to-health-canada/). All of them had authors who had received funding from Red Bull. [The European Union joined in](https://www.documentcloud.org/documents/28519527-comments-from-the-european-union-regarding-notification-regulations-amending-the-food-and-drug-regulations-supplemented-foods/), cautioning against the labeling requirement and pointing to the European Food Safety Authority report.

A year later, Health Canada did an about-face, not only abandoning its proposal to make the warning label permanent, but dropping it altogether. 

In a written statement, Health Canada told The Examination and the Toronto Star that it “relied heavily” on the 2015 European food authority report, as well as its own risk assessments, in making the decision. The agency said it considered “a broad range of information,” including published studies and stakeholder submissions. 

Heather Mitchell called Health Canada’s decision “a clear dereliction of duty.” 

Nearly a decade after she lost her son, energy drink cans in Canada provide less information about alcohol risks than they did on the night he died. 

*Aisvarya Chandrasekar, a computational journalist at the Tow Center for Digital Journalism at Columbia University, contributed to the data analysis.* 

*Léopold Salzenstein, a data reporter at The Examination, and Rachel Auslander, a freelance journalist, contributed reporting.*

## Further Reading
